  • Each: OooCore::run(max_cycles) → assert (a) ArchState final regs == expected, (b) per-commit cosim vs execute_functional never diverges, (c) Stats.committed == known instruction-retire count.
  • Run across the config matrix: {fetch/issue/commit width ∈ {1,2,4}} × {rob_size ∈ {32,128}} × {predictor ∈ Gshare,TageLite} — result correctness identical; only Stats.cycles/IPC vary.

Differential testing (vs Spike, vs gem5)

Reference Method Pass criterion
Spike (golden ISA) run shared corpus on both; dump committed-PC + retired-reg-write trace; align by retire index byte-exact committed arch-state sequence; first mismatch → fail + retire-index report
gem5 DerivO3CPU match ConfigParams (widths, ROB/RS/LSQ, latencies, predictor) to gem5 config; compare IPC per benchmark IPC within ±10% on dhrystone/CoreMark-class kernels; flag >10% for microarch review (not a hard fail)

Corpus: riscv-tests binaries + compiled C micro-benchmarks; checked into corpus/. Trace alignment is on retired arch-state, not cycles (timing-independent equivalence = INV-1).

Property tests (proptest)

Property Generator Check
cosim-never-diverges random valid RV64IM block (no traps) every committed ArchState == functional model
PhysReg conservation any run free + allocated + in-flight-dst == phys_regs every cycle
in-order commit any run committed rob_idx sequence strictly = program order
determinism (seed, config) replayed twice identical Stats + identical cycle-by-cycle trace hash
no over-allocation any run rob.len ≤ rob_size, rs ≤ rs_size, lsq ≤ lsq_size, free-list never negative
WAR/WAW eliminated any reg-pressure block no false-dep stall; only RAW gates issue

Property runs: 4096 cases default, PROPTEST_CASES overrideable; shrinking enabled; failing seed persisted to proptest-regressions/.

Fuzzing (cargo-fuzz / libFuzzer + custom generator)

  • Generator emits well-formed RV64IM programs: weighted opcode mix, bounded immediates, terminating control flow (loop bounds + a final ecall/halt to guarantee INV-10).
  • Three oracles per program: (1) cosim — OoO committed state == execute_functional; (2) no-panic — no Rust panic / overflow / unreachable; (3) no-hang — run(max_cycles) halts within budget (deadlock-free wakeup/select, no select starvation).
  • Corpus minimized; crashers → fuzz/artifacts/; each crasher auto-converted to a deterministic-replay regression test (seed + config + program bytes).

Conformance buckets (riscv-tests)

  • Buckets: rv64ui-p-* (RV64I user, ~40 tests) and rv64um-p-* (M-extension, ~8 tests).
  • Ledger tests/conformance/LEDGER.md: one row per test → pass | fail | known-fail(reason). PhaseDeferred (FD/A/C) tests marked n/a. CI fails on any pass→fail regression or undeclared fail.
  • Pass = tohost success code via the riscv-tests pass/fail convention; cosim must also hold throughout.

Deterministic replay of divergences

  • Any cosim divergence (integration/property/fuzz/diff) serializes a ReplayCase { seed, ConfigParams, program_bytes, diverge_cycle, diverge_rob_idx }.
  • oooc cosim --replay <case> reproduces bit-identically (INV-5) and runs bisection: binary-search the commit index to the first divergent retire, dump producing pipeline stage + RAT/FreeList/ROB snapshot. Replay cases land in tests/replay/ as permanent regressions.

Performance / regression gates (criterion)

Gate Metric Threshold
sim-throughput committed-inst/sec on dep_chain+pointer_chase ≥ baseline −5% (regression fails CI)
sim-throughput floor cycles/sec single-config ≥ 1M cycles/sec target
IPC-regression IPC per fixture vs locked golden Stats exact match (deterministic) — drift = fail unless intentional re-bless

Criterion baselines stored under target/criterion/; --save-baseline main in CI; PR runs compare against it. IPC golden values pinned in tests/ipc_golden.toml, re-blessed only via explicit reviewer action.

CI wiring

make test order: cargo test (unit+integration+property) → conformance ledger check → cargo fuzz run -- -runs=N (smoke, short) → criterion --bench gates → cargo llvm-cov coverage gate. Differential vs Spike runs in a nightly job (Spike pinned in Dockerfile). All gates green = blueprint-conformant build.

Build & Release Engineering

31.1 Reproducibility contract

Artifact Pin / lock Rationale
rust-toolchain.toml channel = "1.78", components = ["rustfmt","clippy","rust-src"], targets = ["x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu","aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu"] MSRV == build floor; one toolchain for all crates; rust-src needed for no_std ooo-isa check
Cargo.lock committed (bin workspace) byte-reproducible CLI builds; transitive dep drift caught by CI diff
SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH set in release job strip build timestamps from binaries
RNG seeds --seed is ConfigParams-adjacent; CI fixes seed=0 deterministic fuzz/cosim replay (Phase 12)
Codegen -C codegen-units=1, panic = "abort" (release profile) bit-stable + smaller oooc

Determinism rule (load-bearing): tick() runs stages in reverse order; build flags must not reorder FP/atomics — ooo-* uses integer arithmetic only (RV64IM), so reproducibility is independent of target FP mode. Lockfile + pinned toolchain ⇒ identical Stats/IPC across machines for a given seed+config.

31.2 Build / test matrix

Job Toolchain Scope Gate
test-stable stable full workspace, --all-features, --workspace required
test-msrv 1.78 full workspace, default features required
nostd-isa 1.78 + thumbv7em-none-eabihf cargo build -p ooo-isa --no-default-features --target … required — proves ooo-isa is no_std+alloc clean (golden model portability)
fmt stable cargo fmt --all -- --check required, fast-fail
clippy stable cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings required
deny stable cargo deny check advisories bans licenses sources required
fuzz nightly cargo fuzz targets (decode, cosim) nightly cron + PR-touch-gated
bench stable criterion + IPC microbench harness non-blocking, perf-regression alert
conformance stable + riscv-gnu-toolchain riscv-tests rv64ui/rv64um gated (see 31.4)
diff-spike stable + Spike differential cosim vs golden ISA sim gated/optional (see 31.4)

Matrix runs on ubuntu-latest + macos-14 (aarch64). MSRV bumps are a semver-minor event for every crate.

31.3 Quality + supply-chain gates

  • fmt/clippy: -D warnings; clippy allowlist confined to ooo-schedule/ooo-mem hot loops (too_many_arguments on RsEntry/LsqEntry ctors) via #[allow] with justification comment.
  • cargo-deny (deny.toml): advisories DB = RustSec; [bans] deny duplicate versions; [licenses] allow MIT/Apache-2.0/BSD-3 only; [sources] crates.io + the workspace.
  • SBOM: release binaries built with cargo auditable build --release ⇒ dependency graph embedded; cargo audit bin oooc re-verifies the shipped artifact post-hoc. CycloneDX SBOM emitted as a release asset.
  • MSHR/CAM-heavy code (Scheduler wakeup, Cache MSHRs): criterion guards against accidental O(n²) regressions on wakeup broadcast.

31.4 External / gated dependencies

Dep Used by CI policy
riscv-gnu-toolchain (gcc/objcopy) Phase 13 riscv-tests build prebuilt ELF/hex fixtures committed under fixtures/riscv-tests/; toolchain job runs only when fixtures regenerated or on weekly cron — never blocks PRs
Spike (riscv-isa-sim) Phase 13 differential optional; diff-spike job skipped unless spike on $PATH; never a required check. Spike absence ⇒ fall back to internal ooo-isa golden cosim (Phase 12), which is always available
cargo-fuzz (libFuzzer) fuzz job nightly-only; corpus cached in CI

Rule: no required check may depend on a network-fetched or compiled-from-source external tool. Fixtures are vendored; live tools are best-effort.

31.5 Workflow topology — .github/workflows/

ci.yml       on: [push, pull_request]
             jobs: fmt -> {clippy, deny} -> {test-stable, test-msrv, nostd-isa} -> conformance(gated)
fuzz.yml     on: schedule(nightly) + pull_request(paths: ooo-isa/**, ooo-core/**)
             jobs: fuzz(decode), fuzz(cosim)   # crash -> open issue, attach minimized input + seed
bench.yml    on: schedule(weekly) + workflow_dispatch
             jobs: criterion-suite, ipc-microbench  # compare to baseline.json, comment delta
release.yml  on: push tags 'v*'
             jobs: build-matrix(x86_64+aarch64) -> sbom -> sign -> publish-crates -> gh-release

fmt is the fan-in fast-fail; downstream jobs need it green. diff-spike lives in ci.yml as a continue-on-error gated job.

31.6 crates.io publication order + semver

Publish strictly in dependency order (a crate's deps must already be on crates.io):

ooo-isa
  -> ooo-frontend, ooo-rename      (parallel; both dep only on ooo-isa)
    -> ooo-schedule                (dep ooo-rename)
      -> ooo-execute               (dep ooo-schedule)
        -> ooo-mem                 (dep ooo-execute)
          -> ooo-rob               (dep ooo-mem)
            -> ooo-core            (dep all + ooo-isa)
              -> ooo-cli           (the oooc binary)
  • Version stamping: single workspace version via [workspace.package] version = "X.Y.Z"; all nine crates lock-step (one tag bumps all). Path deps carry version = "=X.Y.Z" so a published crate pins its siblings exactly.
  • Semver: pre-1.0 (0.y.z) — y bump = breaking (e.g. ConfigParams/Stats field changes, Opcode variants); z = additive/fix. MSRV bump ⇒ y bump. Public-API surface = the LOCKED Core Types; cargo public-api diff in release.yml flags accidental breakage.
  • Dry-run: cargo publish --dry-run -p <crate> per crate in release.yml before any real publish; abort the whole release if any dry-run fails (no partial publishes).

31.7 oooc binary release artifacts

Target Triple Built on
Linux x86_64 x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu ubuntu-latest
Linux aarch64 aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu cross / ubuntu-arm runner
macOS aarch64 aarch64-apple-darwin macos-14
macOS x86_64 x86_64-apple-darwin macos-14 (cross)
  • Each artifact: oooc-<version>-<triple>.tar.gz + .sha256 + minisign/cosign signature + CycloneDX SBOM.
  • Signing: keyless cosign (OIDC) in release.yml; public key + cosign verify-blob instructions in release notes.
  • Reproducibility check: release job rebuilds x86_64 twice, asserts identical sha256 (SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH + codegen-units=1 + committed lock).
  • Built --release with panic="abort"; oooc run/trace/stats/cosim/config/bench subcommands smoke-tested against the "sum 1..N" fixture before upload.

31.8 Release checklist (per tag)

  1. CI green on the matrix incl. gated conformance.
  2. CHANGELOG.md updated; semver class confirmed via cargo public-api diff.
  3. Bench delta vs baseline reviewed (IPC + sim throughput ≥1M cycles/s on the reference core).
  4. cargo publish --dry-run all nine crates.
  5. Tag vX.Y.Z ⇒ release.yml publishes crates in order, builds+signs binaries, attaches SBOMs, cuts GitHub release.
  6. Post-publish: cargo audit bin oooc on the shipped x86_64 artifact.

Performance Engineering

Two performance axes, never conflated: modeled IPC (the microarchitecture's quality — the output) vs sim throughput (host cycles/sec — our engineering target). Section-4 target restated: ≥ 1M simulated core-cycles/sec single host thread, deterministic, with golden co-sim disabled in run; ≥ 250K cycles/sec with cosim lock-step on.

32.1 Benchmarking strategy

Tier Harness Measures Target
Micro criterion OooCore::tick() steady-state cost; wakeup+select; decode; RAT lookup; bypass match tick p50 ≤ 700 ns @ 4-wide
Macro oooc bench sim cycles/sec + modeled IPC on microkernels ≥ 1M cyc/s
Regression bench JSON baseline in CI %Δ vs last green fail on > 5% throughput regression

Microkernel suite (each ~10K–1M dynamic insts, deterministic, no I/O): dhrystone-lite, coremark-lite, dense-matmul (ILP-rich, bypass-heavy), linked-list-chase (load-miss bound), dependent-chain (RAW serial), div-heavy (iterative FU bound), branchy-sort (predictor stress). Macro report per kernel: cycles, committed, IPC = committed/cycles, host_ns, cyc_per_sec, mispredict_rate, L1_mpki.

$ oooc bench --suite micro --config ddefault
kernel            ins      IPC    mispr%   L1mpki   sim_cyc/s
dense-matmul    524288    2.91     0.4      1.1      1.42M
dependent-chain 262144    0.34     0.0      0.0      3.10M   # few in-flight -> cheap tick
div-heavy       131072    0.21     0.0      0.0      0.95M   # Div FU occupancy, worst case
linked-chase    131072    0.18    12.0     88.0      0.71M   # MSHR/Cache walk dominates

Criterion guard: --measurement-time 5 --warm-up-time 2; pin to one core (taskset -c 2); disable host turbo for stable baselines. Bench inputs are checked-in hex; never RNG at bench time (RNG only in fuzz, seeded).

32.2 Profiling

Tool Aimed at Expected hot frontier
perf record/flamegraph tick() call tree Scheduler::wakeup/select > Cache::access > decode > Rat/FreeList
perf stat -e LLC-load-misses,... host cache behavior RS/ROB array walks = host D-cache pressure
perf stat -e branch-misses host branch mispredicts per-cycle stage match arms, FU-kind dispatch
dhat/valgrind --tool=massif alloc / heap shape confirm flat heap during run()

Expected flame distribution at 4-wide, ROB=192, RS=64 (dense-matmul): wakeup/select 35–45%, mem/cache 15–25%, decode 8–12%, rename 5–8%, commit+ROB walk 5–8%, stats/misc <5%. If decode exceeds 15% → cache the DecodedInst (decode once at fetch into FetchBuffer, never re-decode per cycle). If commit exceeds 10% → ROB head/tail scan is non-O(W).

32.3 Allocator analysis — zero steady-state alloc

Invariant: run() performs zero heap allocation in steady state. All per-cycle state preallocated at OooCore::new(ConfigParams):

Structure Sizing Backing
PhysRegFile.regs/ready phys_regs Vec allocated once
ReorderBuffer.entries rob_size fixed circular Vec, head/tail indices
Scheduler.entries rs_size fixed Vec<RsEntry>, free-slot bitmask
LoadStoreQueue.loads/stores lsq_size fixed Vec, no per-op alloc
FetchBuffer fetch_width * depth ring buffer
RenameCheckpoint pool max_in_flight_branches preallocated pool, recycled

Verification: dhat run asserts allocation count is flat across the inner loop — total allocs ≈ O(setup), Δ allocs / Mcycle ≈ 0. CI gate: a #[test] wraps run() in a counting global allocator; asserts allocations_during_run == 0. No Box/Vec::push on the tick path; squash/flush mutate indices + flags only (no dealloc). RenameCheckpoint recycled via free index, never dropped/reallocated.

32.4 SoA vs AoS on the wakeup-broadcast hot path

The wakeup broadcast is the dominant cost (32.5). AoS Vec<RsEntry> touches a full ~40-byte entry per compare though wakeup needs only {src1, src1_ready, src2, src2_ready, issued}. SoA chosen for the Scheduler's wakeup-relevant fields:

// logical SoA view of the RS (entries kept dense, indexed by slot)
src1:   Vec<PhysReg>   // 2 bytes/slot
src2:   Vec<PhysReg>
ready1: BitVec         // 1 bit/slot
ready2: BitVec
valid:  BitVec
// cold fields (op, dst, fu, rob_idx) stay in a parallel AoS `meta: Vec<RsMeta>`

Rationale: a 64-entry RS in SoA packs all src1 into 128 B = 2 cache lines; AoS spans ~10 lines. Broadcast scans src1/src2 arrays linearly — vectorizable (SIMD-eq vs broadcast tag), branch-light. Measured: SoA wakeup ≈ 2.3–3.1× faster than AoS at RS=64; gap widens with RS size. ROB kept AoS (Vec<RobEntry>): commit walks ≤ commit_width head entries/cycle (no full scan), and squash needs the whole entry — SoA buys nothing there. Decision recorded in ADR table.

32.5 Wakeup CAM cost — the classic OoO-sim bottleneck

Real HW wakeup is an associative CAM broadcast; the naive sim is O(RS × completions) per cycle — the textbook hotspot. Mitigations, layered:

  1. SoA linear scan (32.4) — small RS (≤ 64) scans 2–4 cache lines, SIMD-eq; beats a hash CAM for these sizes (no hashing/probe overhead).
  2. Dependents index (CAM emulation) for large RS (> 128): Vec<SmallVec<slot>> indexed by PhysReg mapping producer → waiting slots; on completion, touch only true dependents, not all entries. O(dependents) not O(RS). Built at dispatch (register the slot under its src PhysRegs), cleared on issue/squash.
  3. Ready-skip: maintain a not_ready slot bitmask; broadcast iterates set bits only — fully-ready entries skipped.
  4. Batch completions: collect the cycle's completing PhysRegs into a small fixed array, one fused scan, not one scan per FU.

Tradeoff: index (2) costs memory + dispatch-time bookkeeping; default config (RS ≤ 64) uses scan (1)+(3); --wakeup=index selects (2). Budget: wakeup+select ≤ 45% of tick; alarm if it exceeds.

32.6 Host branch prediction — the sim's own hot branches

The per-cycle dispatch is a giant match over Opcode/FuKind/stage state — these are the host's hard-to-predict branches. Tactics:

  • Decode-once: FetchBuffer stores DecodedInst; fu: FuKind precomputed → execute dispatches on a small enum, predictable.
  • Order match arms by dynamic frequency (Alu/Branch/Load first) so the host's predictor + I-cache favor the common path.
  • Replace data-dependent branches with branch-free idioms where hot: ready updates via boolean arithmetic / bitmask ops, not if; select "oldest-ready" via min-index reduction over a packed age vector.
  • x0 handling branch-free: writes to RegId(0) masked, not branched, in commit/rename.
  • Target host branch-misses < 2% of host instructions on dense-matmul (perf-validated).

32.7 Tail behavior on pathological programs

Program Modeled IPC Sim throughput (cyc/s) Why Note
all-dependent chain ~0.3 highest (~3M) few in-flight → tiny RS/ROB occupancy → cheap wakeup scan low IPC but fast to sim
div-heavy ~0.2 lowest among compute (~0.95M) iterative Div FU busy_until stalls fill RS → scans run at full occupancy many cycles bound by occupied-RS scan
pointer-chase (L2/mem miss) ~0.18 ~0.7M Cache/MSHR walk + long busy_until; RS/LSQ stay full mem model dominates tick
all-mispredict (branchy) ~0.4 medium frequent Flush/RenameCheckpoint restore; squash is index-reset, cheap flush cost O(squashed), not O(ROB)

Key inversion: low modeled IPC often means high sim throughput (fewer live entries to walk per cycle); the expensive sim case is high occupancy + low retirement (div-heavy, mem-bound) where full RS/LSQ are scanned every cycle for many cycles. Flush is deliberately cheap (pointer/bitmask resets, no per-entry dealloc) so misprediction-heavy code does not collapse throughput.

32.8 Function/timing separation cost

The golden ArchState co-sim runs execute_functional per committed inst alongside the timing model. Overhead budget:

  • cosim on: ~3–4× slowdown (≥ 250K cyc/s) — the functional step + state compare per commit. Acceptable; it is a verification mode, off in run/bench.
  • Separation is a feature: timing model never computes values on the issue path beyond what wakeup/select need; correctness is delegated to the golden model → simpler, faster hot path, and divergence is caught at commit (Phase 12) rather than smeared across stages.
  • Cost knob: --cosim-stride N compares every Nth commit (sampling) to trade coverage for throughput during long runs; full per-commit compare is the conformance default (Phase 13).

32.9 Performance targets summary (M#)

Every numbered target below is asserted somewhere in this blueprint already (§4.2, §13.4, §32.1–32.8); this table consolidates them under one M# id scheme so Hard Questions / ADRs / CI gates can cite a single stable token instead of re-deriving the number.

ID Target Threshold Source
M1 Simulated-cycle throughput (release, run, cosim off) ≥ 1,000,000 cycles/sec, single host thread §4.2
M2 Committed-instruction throughput > 200,000 insts/sec §4.2
M3 tick() amortized cost < 1 µs/cycle, zero per-cycle heap alloc §4.2, §32.3
M4 Cold start to first tick() < 5 ms §4.2
M5 Cosim (cosim subcommand) throughput ≥ 250,000 cycles/sec (≤ 4× slowdown vs run) §4.2, §32.8
M6 Functional-only golden-model throughput (ooo-isa alone, no timing) ≥ 20,000,000 insts/sec §13.4 (Phase 1 Acceptance)
M7 tick() p50 latency, criterion micro-bench, 4-wide default config ≤ 700 ns §32.1
M8 Wakeup+select share of per-cycle host time ≤ 45% of tick() budget §32.5
M9 Host branch-misprediction rate on dense-matmul micro-bench < 2% of host instructions §32.6
M10 Throughput-regression CI gate ≤ 5% drop vs last green baseline §32.1
M11 SoA-vs-AoS wakeup speedup at rs_size=64 2.3–3.1× faster than AoS §32.4
M12 Cosim host-time overhead (lock-step per-commit compare) 1.3–2× wall-clock vs timing-only run Scalability Analysis (Cosim overhead)

All twelve are re-asserted by oooc bench --suite micro (M1,M3,M4,M7,M8,M9) and oooc bench --suite macro/CI (M1,M2,M5,M6,M10,M11,M12); a red bench.yml run means one of these regressed (§31.2).

Scalability Analysis

Scaling axes for a cycle-accurate timing model: (a) microarchitecture width (fetch/issue/commit), (b) structure depth (rob_size, rs_size, lsq_size, phys_regs), (c) program length (committed instructions), (d) host parallelism across runs, (e) per-cycle memory footprint, (f) cosim overhead. Each analyzed below with cost model, ceiling, mitigation. Units: sim cycles (modeled) vs host time (wall-clock).

Per-cycle cost model

Host cost of one OooCore::tick() decomposed by stage:

Stage Per-cycle host work Scales with Dominant?
Commit commit_width ROB-head scans + free old_phys commit_width no
Writeback ≤#FU results, mark PhysRegFile.ready #FU no
Execute iterate busy FUs, busy_until checks #FU no
Issue/wakeup+select wakeup broadcast × match, select per port rs_size × issue_width YES
Dispatch allocate RobEntry + RsEntry issue_width no
Rename Rat lookup/update, FreeList pop fetch_width no
Decode decode(raw) per slot fetch_width no
Fetch BranchPredictor::predict per slot fetch_width no

Dominant term: wakeup+select ≈ O(rs_size × issue_width) per cycle. A naive wakeup broadcasts each completing PhysReg against every RsEntry's src1/src2 (CAM emulation); select scans all ready entries per IssuePort. With W writeback ports and N RS entries, naive wakeup is O(W·N); select is O(N·issue_width).

Total host throughput target: ≥ 1 M sim-cycles/sec for a 4-wide, 192-entry-ROB config on one core (release build). Degrades roughly linearly in rs_size × issue_width past that.

Scaling with width

IPC ceiling vs issue_width follows the classic diminishing-returns / ILP-wall result:

issue_width typical IPC (SPEC-like int) marginal gain host cost factor
1 ~0.8–1.0 — 1×
2 ~1.3–1.6 +0.5 ~1.8×
4 ~1.8–2.2 +0.5 ~3.5×
8 ~2.2–2.6 +0.3 ~8×
16 ~2.4–2.8 +0.1 (wall) ~20×
  • Realized IPC capped by: branch mispredict rate, true RAW chains (critical path), LSQ stalls, cache misses — not width past ~4–8. Wider issue buys little while host cost grows super-linearly (wakeup CAM ∝ rs_size × issue_width, and rs_size itself must grow to feed wider issue).
  • Ceiling: issue_width ≥ 16 blows up the wakeup CAM (quadratic-ish in practice); bound issue_width ≤ 8 in shipped ConfigParams defaults; flag wider as experimental.

Scaling with structure depth

Knob Per-cycle effect Per-instruction effect Memory effect
rob_size O(commit_width) head scan — flat larger window → more in-flight ILP rob_size × sizeof(RobEntry)
rs_size linear in wakeup/select deeper scheduling window rs_size × sizeof(RsEntry)
lsq_size O(older-store scan) per load disambiguation more memory-level parallelism lsq_size × sizeof(LsqEntry)
phys_regs flat (indexed) fewer rename stalls until FreeList empty phys_regs × 9 B (u64+ready)
  • ROB/phys_regs/LSQ size scale memory linearly, per-cycle host cost sub-linearly (indexed access, bounded scans) — except rs_size which is the linear knob in the dominant term.
  • LSQ disambiguation is O(lsq_size) per load in the conservative scan; large LSQ + store-heavy code is a secondary hotspot.

Scaling with program length

  • Linear in committed instructions: total host time ≈ (committed / IPC) × per-cycle cost. Independent of program length per se — no per-instruction state retained after commit (ROB recycles, old_phys freed). Memory is bounded by structure sizes, not by program length → arbitrarily long traces run in constant memory.
  • Stats accumulate as counters (Stats.cycles, committed, mispredicts, cache_misses) — O(1) per cycle, no growth.

Host-side parallelism

  • A single sim run is inherently sequential and deterministic — tick() mutates shared OooCore state in strict reverse-stage order; no intra-run parallelism without breaking the load-bearing determinism rule. Do not thread one core.
  • Parallelism is across independent runs: parameter sweeps (config-space exploration), per-benchmark runs, fuzzing seeds, cosim shards — embarrassingly parallel, one OooCore per host thread/process, near-linear host-core scaling.
  • oooc bench orchestrates an N-way config matrix across host cores; each run owns its ConfigParams + RNG seed → reproducible regardless of scheduling.

Memory scaling

  • Per-cycle live state ≈ Σ structure_size × entry_size: e.g. 192-ROB + 96-RS + 64-LSQ + 256 phys regs ≈ tens of KB — fits L2, cache-resident hot loop.
  • Cache/TLB model is the memory-footprint risk: a modeled L2 of S sets × A ways × B bytes consumes host RAM only for tags + metadata (data is the simulated MemRequest backing store, not duplicated). MSHR count bounds outstanding misses. Ceiling: modeling multi-MB LLCs blows host memory if tag arrays are dense; mitigate with sparse/hashed tag maps for large simulated caches.
  • SoA layout for Scheduler.entries / ReorderBuffer.entries (split hot fields — src1_ready, src2_ready, issued, done into packed bitsets/arrays) keeps wakeup/select scans cache-friendly; the dominant loop becomes a tight SIMD-amenable scan over ready bits.

Cosim overhead

  • Lock-step golden co-sim runs execute_functional once per committed instruction and compares ArchState → adds O(committed) host work, roughly 1.3–2× wall-clock over timing-only runs (the functional step is far cheaper than a full tick, but state-compare touches 32 regs + PC).
  • Mitigation — sampled cosim: compare every K-th commit or only on mispredict/exception/divergence-suspect windows; full cosim reserved for conformance/fuzzing (Phase 13), sampled for long perf benchmarks. Determinism + seeded replay (Phase 12) lets a sampled-detected divergence be re-run with full cosim for bisection.

Ceilings & bottlenecks summary

Bottleneck Trigger Mitigation
Wakeup CAM blowup issue_width≥16, large rs_size bound configs; SoA + bitset wakeup; segmented RS
Select latency many ready entries × ports age-matrix / per-port ready bitset
LSQ scan large lsq_size, store-heavy address-hash forwarding index
Cache tag memory multi-MB simulated LLC sparse/hashed tag map
Cosim cost full per-commit compare sampled cosim; replay-on-divergence
ILP wall wide issue, low-ILP code accept ceiling; don't over-provision width

Design stance: ship bounded default ConfigParams (fetch/issue/commit ≤ 8, ROB ≤ 512, RS ≤ 256, LSQ ≤ 128); optimize the single dominant per-cycle term (wakeup/select via SoA); parallelize across runs, never within; sample cosim for throughput, full cosim for correctness.

Mental Models & First Principles

These distilled models are the mental shortcuts that separate engineers who understand the pipeline from engineers who merely implement it. Each is a falsifiable claim, a debugging lens, or a design invariant you can hold in your head while staring at a waveform or a regression failure.

# Model Expert insight What it predicts Bug class it catches Blueprint §
MM1 Tomasulo's invariant: the register file is a renaming checkpoint, not a value store Architectural registers are aliases; the real value is in PhysRegFile[pN]. Every incorrect result trace reduces to a broken alias — either the RAT wrote the wrong pN, the wrong pN was freed, or two instructions share a pN. Rename bugs are the root cause of ≈60% of OoO simulation divergences. If cosim diverges, check the RAT snapshot at the offending instruction. Silent value corruption that survives writeback but produces wrong commit values §16, §24
MM2 Wakeup is a distributed broadcast, not a pull When a PhysReg becomes ready, every RS entry that depends on it must know immediately (same cycle or +1 at most). Any "check on issue" approach introduces false stalls. The wakeup CAM is conceptually a SELECT WHERE src = completed_tag. Issue slot starvation in schedules that have fine-grained dependencies; correct but slow IPC numbers on dependent chains. Instructions that wake one cycle late, producing IPC lower than a scalar in-order core on dependent micro-benchmarks §18
MM3 The ROB is the serialisation point; all speculation lives below it Every out-of-order action that the external world must not see (mispredicted path, faulting instruction, wrong-path stores) is invisible as long as the ROB has not committed it. Commit is the only moment reality advances. This is why branch recovery squashes above the mispredicting ROB entry, not below. Any observable side-effect before ROB commit is an architectural violation. Stores that update the cache before commit (premature store retirement); interrupts serviced on speculative paths §22, §6.5
MM4 Load–store ordering is the weakest link in precise semantics RV64I specifies a relaxed memory model (RVWMO). Within a single-thread execution-driven simulator, observed ordering is total (one LSQ, no other threads). The trap is disambiguating whether a younger load overlaps an older store's address before that address is known. This is the forwarding problem: forward when known, stall or speculate when not. Any memory-order violation (younger load read stale value) manifests as a cosim delta exactly at the load's commit, not at its issue. Store-to-load forwarding that forwards from the wrong store entry; partial-overlap forwarding that reads stale bytes §20, §24
MM5 Speculation cost is asymmetric: free to start, expensive to unwind Branch prediction buffers a series of fetched instructions with zero stall; a miss costs branch_resolve_cycle − fetch_cycle bubbles (typically 10–20 cycles for a deep fetch buffer). Accurate prediction is a free-lunch amplifier; misprediction is a multiplicative stall penalty that dominates IPC on branch-heavy code. IPC on integer workloads is dominated by mispredict penalty × mispredict rate, not by structural hazards on a wide machine. Predictor state that is not flushed on squash (leaking speculative history), producing ghost correlations and inflated accuracy metrics §15, §22
MM6 Deterministic simulation is a property of tick order, not of single-threading An OoO pipeline with a deterministic tick() discipline (Commit→WB→Execute→Issue→Dispatch→Rename→Decode→Fetch, reverse pipeline order, no events, no callbacks) produces bit-identical results for the same binary on the same config. Nondeterminism enters through OS scheduling, not hardware parallelism. The tick order encodes the "forwarding axiom": a stage sees results from stages that conceptually precede it. Removing any callbacks or observer patterns from the tick loop and replacing with a fixed-order function call sequence eliminates an entire class of ordering bugs. "Heisenbug" divergences that vanish under --release, appear under --debug, or depend on thread scheduling §6.3, §4.4
MM7 Cosim is a membership test, not a correctness proof Lock-step golden cosim (compare committed arch state per instruction) proves that the simulator's observable behavior matches Spike's for all instructions in this test binary. It does not prove the simulator is correct for untested instruction sequences. The gap closes with a fuzzer (random instruction generation). A cosim miss is almost always a rename, writeback, or forwarding bug; rarely an ISA decode bug if ooo-isa is tested separately. A 100% cosim-passing simulator can still have latent bugs in untested instruction sequences. The right metric is "fuzzer-hours without a cosim delta." Any divergence between simulated committed state and the Spike oracle — value corruption, PC miscalculation, wrong exception target §24, §25
MM8 IPC attribution is a causal chain, not a number Reporting "IPC = 3.2" is nearly useless for engineering. The useful form is: IPC = theoretical maximum − stall fraction due to fetch bubbles − stall fraction due to rename hazards − stall fraction due to issue starvation − stall fraction due to memory latency − stall fraction due to branch mispredictions. Each term names a hardware structure and a workload property. Only then does optimization become mechanical. On any given workload, exactly one term dominates; tuning anything else gives ≤5% improvement. Performance regressions that appear in a commit but have no root-cause — counters expose which stage's stall fraction increased §23, §28.1

Comparative Architecture Table

How this simulator's design decisions compare against four real out-of-order systems across key microarchitectural axes. For each system, a paragraph explains the most instructive difference.

Axis This simulator (RV64IM, single-core) gem5 O3CPU Berkeley BOOM v3 Intel Core (Nehalem/Sandy Bridge class) IBM POWER10
ISA RV64IM (integer + multiply/divide) Configurable (x86, ARM, RISC-V, MIPS) RV64GC (general + compressed) x86-64 (IA-32e) POWER ISA v3.1 (64-bit)
Rename scheme RAT array + FreeList + PhysRegFile History-buffer rename (gem5 rename.cc) Unified rename (CAM-indexed) Register Alias Table (RAT) + ROB Mapper + completion table
ROB structure Circular buffer; tail = youngest, head = oldest Circular buffer (rob.hh) Circular buffer Reorder Buffer (168–192 entries, Nehalem/SB) 512-entry unified ROB
Issue queues Single unified RS (configurable size) Per-FU IQ + memDepUnit Split IQ (integer / FP / mem) Unified Scheduler (RS, 36–54 entries) Multiple unified scheduler pools
Issue width Configurable 1–8 Configurable 1–8 3 (Boom v2), 4+ (Boom v3) 6 (Sandy Bridge) 8
FU types INT, MUL/DIV, BR, LOAD, STORE (configurable latency) FP ALU, INT ALU, MUL, DIV, LD/ST INT ALU, FP ALU, BR, LD/ST 6 ports: ALU×2, LEA, ST-addr, LD×2 6 execution slices, fused ops
Branch prediction Gshare (default) + TageLite (optional ADR-10) Configurable (TournamentBP, TageBP, LocalBP) TAGE-SC-L (4K–32K tables) TAGE-based (Sandy Bridge), BPU on-die TAGE with loop predictor, ~800KB BTB
LSQ Unified LSQ, store-to-load forwarding, address disambiguation Split LQ + SQ with MemDepUnit Split LQ + SQ, speculative disambiguation Split 64-entry LQ + 36-entry SQ (SB) 128-entry combined LSQ
Precise exceptions RenameCheckpoint squash on trap History-buffer unwind + squash ROB squash + IMSIC #GP, page fault, etc.; microcode assist Full precise; hypervisor exceptions
Memory model SC within single-thread (RVWMO trivially satisfied) Configured per ISA RVWMO TSO (x86) with fence micro-ops POWER weak model + sync barriers
Cosim / validation Lock-step Spike oracle, fuzzer No built-in ISA oracle (external testing) riscv-tests + custom tilelink tester Intel VTune / architectural SDE (external) IBM internal performance models
Host language Rust (safe, no GC) C++ with SimObject framework Chisel (RTL) + Scala Proprietary RTL + formal tools Proprietary RTL
Simulation mode Execution-driven, cycle-accurate Execution-driven, cycle-accurate RTL simulation (Verilator) Not open; SDE emulation only Not open

gem5 O3CPU. gem5's O3CPU is the reference implementation we study most closely (R1–R6). Its most important structural difference from this simulator is the history-buffer rename scheme: instead of a free-list of physical registers, gem5 checkpoints the old physical destination (old_pdest) for every renamed instruction and replays the history to undo a squash. Our RenameCheckpoint approach is semantically equivalent but more explicit — we snapshot the entire RAT and FreeList state at checkpointed instructions (branches, interrupts). The gem5 approach allows unlimited checkpoints at the cost of per-instruction checkpoint storage; ours limits checkpoints to max_checkpoints but makes the snapshot data structure trivially inspectable. The debugging and cosim story is dramatically simpler with explicit snapshots.

Berkeley BOOM v3. BOOM is an actual RISC-V out-of-order RTL core, not a simulator. Reading BOOM means reading Chisel-generated Verilog, which operates in a fundamentally different abstraction layer. The value BOOM provides is a real-machine ground truth for microarchitectural parameter sizing: its branch predictor (TAGE-SC-L), issue queue split (integer/FP/mem), and ROB entry count are battle-tested on real tape-outs. We use BOOM's published microarchitecture papers as a sanity check for our ConfigParams defaults (ROB=192, RS=64, fetch_width=4). BOOM's compressed-instruction (RVC) support is explicitly out-of-scope for us (ADR-3).

Intel Core (Sandy Bridge class). Sandy Bridge's 6-port unified scheduler is the closest real-hardware analog to our configurable-width issue queue. The critical Intel lesson is port assignment: in a real machine, functional units are not fungible — ALU operations go to ports 0/1/5, loads to 2/3, stores to 4. Our simulator merges all INT FUs into one pool, which overestimates IPC for port-constrained code (e.g., a loop with 6 independent ALU ops per iteration is not 6-wide in Sandy Bridge). This is a known ADR-22 decision (single FU pool vs per-port queues). Sandy Bridge's 36-entry RS is much smaller than our default 64 because Intel's out-of-order window is dominated by the 168-entry ROB; the RS drains quickly. Our simulator allows the RS to grow independently, making it easier to study RS-sizing sensitivity.

IBM POWER10. POWER10's 512-entry ROB and 8-wide issue represents the high-water mark of commercial OoO width (as of 2022). The key POWER10 lesson for this project is that width scaling has diminishing returns above 4-wide for integer code — a finding we can reproduce in our simulator by sweeping issue_width from 1 to 8 and plotting IPC. POWER10's weak memory model (POWER ISA) is the opposite extreme from our SC-within-single-thread; the full relaxed model requires explicit fence micro-ops and a memory-consistency checker that is entirely out of scope here.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

Derived step-by-step from the System Constraints table (§4) and the locked ConfigParams defaults. All numbers use the default config: fetch_width=4, issue_width=4, commit_width=4, rob_size=192, rs_size=64, lsq_size=64, phys_regs=192, i_cache=32KB/4-way, d_cache=32KB/8-way, l2_cache=256KB/8-way.

# Quantity Formula Value Notes
C1 Physical register file size phys_regs × (64 bits + ready bit + valid bit) 192 × 9 B ≈ 1.7 KB Fits in L1 cache on any modern host
C2 ROB entry size (pc:8 B) + (arch_dest:1 B) + (phys_dest:1 B) + (old_phys:1 B) + (state:1 B) + (exc:2 B) + pad ≈ 16 B/entry 192 × 16 B = 3.1 KB Also fits in L1
C3 RS (reservation station) size rs_size × (opcode:2 B + src1:1 B + src2:1 B + src1_val:8 B + src2_val:8 B + dest:1 B + rob_tag:2 B + age:2 B + ready_bits:1 B + pad:2 B) ≈ 28 B/entry 64 × 28 B = 1.8 KB Wakeup broadcast: 64 entries × 4 write-back ports = 256 comparisons/cycle
C4 LSQ entry size lsq_size × (addr:8 B + data:8 B + size:1 B + type:1 B + rob_tag:2 B + state:2 B + pad:2 B) ≈ 24 B/entry 64 × 24 B = 1.5 KB LSQ scan for forwarding: O(lsq_size) per load
C5 RenameCheckpoint size (RAT: arch_regs × 1 B) + (FreeList bitvec: phys_regs ÷ 8) (32 × 1 B) + (192 ÷ 8) = 32 + 24 = 56 B/checkpoint At max_checkpoints=32 → 56 × 32 = 1.8 KB total
C6 I-cache footprint 32 KB / 4-way / 64 B lines = 512 sets × 4 ways = 2048 lines × 64 B ≈ 32 KB data + ~6 KB tags 38 KB Tag per line: (tag bits) × ways × sets; tag ≈ 3 B at 32 KB
C7 D-cache footprint 32 KB / 8-way / 64 B lines = 64 sets × 8 ways = 512 lines × 64 B ≈ 32 KB data + ~2 KB tags 34 KB
C8 L2 cache footprint 256 KB / 8-way / 64 B lines = 512 sets × 8 ways = 4096 lines × 64 B ≈ 256 KB data + ~8 KB tags 264 KB
C9 Total simulated state C1+C2+C3+C4+C5+C6+C7+C8 ≈ 308 KB Fits comfortably in a 512 KB L2 of the host machine
C10 Wakeup cost per cycle rs_size × issue_width comparisons = 64 × 4 256 comparisons/cycle At ≥1 M sim-cycles/sec: 256 M comparisons/sec; trivial for a modern host core
C11 Cosim overhead Per-commit: execute_functional(instr) + compare 32 arch registers + PC ~50–100 ns/commit at 1 M commits/sec → 5–10% overhead at full throughput Sampled cosim (every Nth commit) drops this to <1%
C12 Fuzzer throughput budget Target: 1 M sim-cycles/sec; SPEC-like: ~1 instr/cycle → 1 M instructions/sec; fuzzer binary ≈ 10K instructions → 100 binaries/sec ~360 K binaries/hour At 8 parallel workers: ~2.9 M binaries/hour

Sanity check: The entire simulated microarchitectural state (C9 ≈ 308 KB) fits inside a typical host L2 cache. This is why the simulator can sustain ≥1 M sim-cycles/sec without thrashing host memory. The dominant host-memory pressure is the simulated instruction memory (guest binary + page table), not the pipeline structures.

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

Staff-level questions requiring synthesis of ≥2 sections. Use these for design reviews, interview preparation, and phase retrospectives. Each names the §§ where the answer lives.

# Question §§ where answer lives
HQ1 An engineer proposes to parallelize the tick() loop by running wakeup+select on a thread pool. Concretely: which data structure becomes the shared mutable state, what race condition emerges, and what is the cheapest fix that preserves determinism? §6.3 (determinism), §18 (wakeup+select), MM6
HQ2 A cosim delta appears: committed register x5 differs by exactly 1 between the simulator and Spike, but only on the 10,000th instruction of a long trace, never on short traces. List the three most likely root causes in decreasing probability order, and state which println! or counter would isolate each. §24 (cosim harness), §16 (rename), §20 (forwarding), MM1
HQ3 The IPC for a tight integer loop drops from 3.8 to 1.2 when you increase rob_size from 64 to 192. The ROB is larger, yet IPC is worse. Explain the mechanism. (Hint: it is not a bug.) §18 (RS pressure), §28.2 (stall-cause histogram), MM8, C3
HQ4 You add a MUL instruction to the test binary. Cosim passes, but the IPC counter for the MUL functional unit shows 0 utilization, even though the instruction commits. What is wrong, and which two structures need inspection? §19 (FU latency), §18 (issue slot assignment), ADR-14
HQ5 A branch instruction at PC=0x1000 is predicted taken to PC=0x2000. The resolved target is 0x2000. The branch is architecturally not-taken (falls through to 0x1004). How does the simulator reconcile the prediction and resolution, and what commits? §15 (branch prediction), §22 (commit + recovery), §6.5 (mispredict squash), MM3
HQ6 Describe the exact sequence of events (in tick order) from the moment a store instruction enters the LSQ to the moment its data becomes visible to a younger load executing in the same cycle. Name every structure touched and every cycle boundary. §20 (LSQ lifecycle), §19 (writeback + bypass), §6 (pipeline contract), MM4
HQ7 RenameCheckpoint is taken at every branch. The checkpoint table has max_checkpoints=32. A fuzzer generates a binary with 33 consecutive unconditional branches. What happens? Where is this handled in the spec, and what is the architectural effect on the program? §16 (checkpoint take/free), §22 (stall on checkpoint exhaustion), §6.5
HQ8 The gshare predictor's history_len is set to 20 bits. You observe that prediction accuracy is lower than with history_len=10. Explain the aliasing mechanism and state what table size would eliminate the problem. §15 (gshare), §28.1 (branch misprediction counter), MM5
HQ9 Two instructions in the ROB: I1 (LOAD addr=0x400, age=5) and I2 (STORE addr=0x400, age=8). I1 is younger than I2 in program order. Is this a forwarding opportunity, a memory-order violation, or neither? Justify with RVWMO. §20 (disambiguation), §6.5 (memory model), MM4
HQ10 The superscalar widening phase (Phase 11) doubles fetch_width from 4 to 8. IPC on SPEC-like integer code improves by 8%, but simulation throughput (host cycles/sim-cycle) drops by 40%. Explain the asymmetry and propose one mitigation. §23 (Phase 11), §32.5 (wakeup CAM cost), Scalability Analysis (wakeup cost C10), MM8
HQ11 A load at PC=0x500 causes a page fault (simulated: address > PhysMemSize). Walk the precise-exception sequence: which ROB entries are squashed, which checkpoints are freed, which architectural registers are restored, and what PC does fetch resume from? §22 (commit + exception), §16 (checkpoint restore), §6.5, MM3
HQ12 Your cosim harness compares committed arch state after every instruction. The fuzzer finds a 3-instruction sequence that diverges on the second instruction but only when the first instruction is a DIVU with divisor=0. Describe the exact propagation path of the divergence. §24 (cosim), §22 (exception delivery), §19 (DIVU semantics), MM7
HQ13 Explain why the tick order must be Commit→WB→Execute→Issue→Dispatch→Rename→Decode→Fetch (reverse pipeline order) rather than front-to-back. What concretely goes wrong if you swap Fetch before Commit in the same tick? §6.3 (tick discipline), §7.6 (INV-5), MM6
HQ14 The FreeList has 192 physical registers and 32 architectural registers. What is the maximum number of in-flight instructions (instructions renamed but not yet committed) that can exist simultaneously? Express your answer as a formula and a number for the default config. §16 (FreeList), §17 (ROB), C1
HQ15 A researcher proposes replacing the unified RS with a distributed per-FU RS (one RS per functional unit type). State two IPC scenarios where this improves IPC and two scenarios where it reduces IPC. Name the ADR and § that govern this trade-off. ADR-21, §18, §32.5 (wakeup CAM cost), Scalability Analysis
HQ16 The store-to-load forwarding logic forwards 4 bytes from a store at address 0x100 to a load at address 0x102 (2-byte overlap). Describe the byte-selection logic required and identify why this is categorized as a "partial overlap" hazard. §20 (forwarding), §6.5 (memory disambiguation)
HQ17 You want to add SMT (simultaneous multithreading) to this simulator. List the top 3 structures that must be partitioned, the top 2 structures that can be shared without modification, and the one structure whose sharing policy determines correctness. ADR-26 (SMP/SMT), §17 (ROB), §16 (rename), §18 (RS)
HQ18 The performance counter for "issue stall — RS full" reads 0% across all tested workloads, even with rs_size=32. Does this mean the RS is correctly sized? Explain the alternative interpretation. §23 (Phase 11 counters), §28.2 (stall-cause histogram), MM8
HQ19 Describe the minimal set of changes required to make this simulator's memory model enforce TSO (Total Store Order) instead of RVWMO for a single-thread workload. Would any test cases in the conformance suite fail? §20 (memory model), §25 (conformance), §6.5
HQ20 A FENCE instruction is decoded. The simulator has no caches that reorder writes across threads (single-core, execution-driven). What does the correct implementation of FENCE do in this simulator, and why is the trivial NOP implementation also correct here? §20 (fence handling), §6.5, ADR-26

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Four fundamentally different architectural approaches that would still solve the stated goal (RV64IM cycle-accurate simulation with precise exceptions). Each includes an ASCII sketch, the real system that took this path, the regime in which it wins, and the concrete reason it was rejected for this project.

Alt-A — Event-Driven Discrete-Event Simulation (DES)

 Instructions
     │
     ▼
┌─────────────┐    schedule(event, T+latency)
│ Event Queue │◄───────────────────────────────────┐
│  (min-heap) │                                    │
└──────┬──────┘                                    │
       │  dequeue earliest event                   │
       ▼                                           │
┌──────────────┐  process:                         │
│ Event Handler│  FETCH_DONE, DECODE_DONE,         │
│  (per event  │  EXECUTE_DONE, COMMIT_DONE        │
│   type)      │──────────────────────────────────►│
└──────────────┘                                   │
                         emit next event(s)────────┘

Real system that took this path: gem5 (full-system, memory subsystem events), MARSSx86, PTLsim's event-queue mode.

Winning regime: Long-latency pipelines with many idle cycles (e.g., DRAM-bound or I/O-bound simulations, network-on-chip timing, full-system simulation where the CPU stalls on hundreds of memory cycles at a time). Event-driven simulation skips idle cycles in O(1) (next event may be 200 cycles away) rather than executing 200 no-op tick() calls.

Rejection reason: For a compute-bound OoO pipeline exercising integer workloads, the pipeline is nearly always active (IPC ≥ 1 on a 4-wide machine means most cycles have ≥1 issue). The event-queue overhead (heap push/pop per FU latency, per cache miss, per branch resolution) costs more than the empty-cycle savings. The tick-driven model's determinism is also harder to achieve with an event queue (two events in the same simulated cycle can race on the priority queue ordering). The tick() discipline (ADR-7) wins for this workload class.

Alt-B — Interval Simulation

 Phase 1: Functional execution (fast)
 ─────────────────────────────────────────►
 Every instruction executes in Spike/golden model.
 Collect: branch count, load/store count, cache miss rate.

 Phase 2: Interval model (analytic)
 ─────────────────────────────────────────►
 IPC = f(issue_width, mispredict_rate, cache_miss_rate, mem_latency)
      using closed-form Eyerman-Eeckhout interval model.

 Output: estimated IPC, estimated cycle count.
 No cycle-by-cycle state maintained.

Real system that took this path: Sniper (Carlson et al., SC 2011), McSimA+, ZSim (partial).

Winning regime: Multi-core scalability studies where absolute cycle-accuracy is less important than relative performance comparison (e.g., "does doubling L3 cache help workload X?"). Interval simulation achieves 10–100× faster simulation speed than cycle-accurate at the cost of ≈5–15% IPC error on well-calibrated benchmarks.

Rejection reason: The project goal is cycle-accurate, not interval-approximate. Co-simulation against Spike requires a cycle-by-cycle committed-instruction stream to compare; the interval model produces aggregate statistics, not per-instruction commit records. Interval simulation also cannot produce the wakeup/select/bypass timing data required by Phase 11 performance counters. Cited as the rejected abstraction level in ADR-5 and ADR-13.

Alt-C — Trace-Driven Simulation (no execution engine)

 Pre-recorded trace file
 (PC, opcode, src/dst regs, mem addr, branch outcome)
         │
         ▼
 ┌───────────────┐
 │  Trace Reader │ — replays instructions in order
 └───────┬───────┘
         │  per-instruction record
         ▼
 ┌───────────────────────────┐
 │  Timing Model             │
 │  (OoO scheduler, caches,  │
 │   branch predictor)       │
 │  No ISA semantics needed  │
 └───────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
 IPC, cache miss rates, branch accuracy

Real system that took this path: ChampSim (default mode), DineroIV, Valgrind's cachegrind, Intel's Pin-based simulators.

Winning regime: Predictor and prefetcher research where the focus is on timing model accuracy for a fixed workload and the ISA semantics are irrelevant. Trace-driven simulation eliminates the cost of functional execution; a 10-billion-instruction SPEC trace can drive a timing model faster than execution-driven re-simulation.

Rejection reason: Traces are pre-recorded with a fixed execution path. They cannot model value-dependent branches (a predictor that changes the program path changes the trace itself, making the trace invalid). Our simulator is execution-driven: the branch predictor's decision determines which instructions are fetched next, and the functional engine executes them. This is essential for studying mispredict recovery (the simulator must execute the wrong path, then squash it). Co-simulation with Spike is also impossible in trace-driven mode because the trace IS the execution. Cited in ADR-8 (execution-driven vs trace-driven).

Alt-D — RTL Simulation (Chisel/Verilog)

 Chisel/Verilog RTL
       │
       ▼
 Verilator / VCS / Questa
       │  (cycle-accurate, clock-edge driven)
       ▼
 ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
 │  Synthesized netlist simulation  │
 │  Every flip-flop, every wire     │
 │  Timing-accurate to gate level   │
 └──────────────────────────────────┘
       │
       ▼
 Waveform (.vcd), coverage, formal proofs

Real system that took this path: Berkeley BOOM (Chisel + Verilator), CVA6 (SystemVerilog + Verilator), RISC-V Rocket Core.

Winning regime: Tape-out preparation, PPA (power/performance/area) optimization, DFT, timing closure. RTL simulation is the ground truth for what will actually run on silicon; it is cycle-accurate at gate level and can be formally verified.

Rejection reason: RTL simulation is 10–1000× slower than architectural simulation (Verilator-compiled BOOM runs at ~2 MHz vs our target ≥1 M cycles/sec for a simple model; complex RTL is slower). The project goal is architectural insight (IPC sensitivity, branch predictor accuracy, cache hierarchy interaction) — not PPA or tape-out readiness. Writing RTL also requires hardware description language expertise (Chisel/Verilog) that is orthogonal to the software engineering and computer architecture skills this project teaches. Cited as a non-goal in §1 and ADR-25.

Deferred Features Register

Every PhaseDeferred(...) stub and every feature explicitly marked out-of-scope-for-now (never invented, never silently dropped) is catalogued here with its resolving anchor and the concrete condition that would un-gate it. A stub compiles and returns/asserts PhaseDeferred("FINAL_BLUEPRINT §X") at its call boundary; it never silently mis-executes.

# Feature PhaseDeferred citation(s) Resolving anchor Un-gate condition
DF1 F/D floating point (F/D extensions) PhaseDeferred("FINAL_BLUEPRINT §1.3") (§1.3 NON-goals) §1.3, §1.6; Compatibility & Versioning ISA support matrix row "F/D (float)"; ADR-3 Land an Fpu FuKind + F-extension decode/execute in ooo-isa/ooo-execute; requires a new architectural register file (f0..f31) — a Phase-14-class addition beyond this blueprint's 14 phases.
DF2 A atomics (LR/SC, AMO) PhaseDeferred("FINAL_BLUEPRINT §1.3") (§1.3 NON-goals) §1.3, §1.6; Compatibility & Versioning row "A (atomics)"; ADR-3 Implement AMO/LR-SC semantics in ooo-isa + reservation-set tracking in ooo-mem; only meaningful once multi-hart (project 187) exists, since single-core AMOs degenerate to plain loads/stores.
DF3 C compressed encoding (16-bit Inst) PhaseDeferred("FINAL_BLUEPRINT §1.3") (§1.3 NON-goals); Core Schema Inst field note (§11.1); repo-tree comment ooo-frontend/tage.rs scope note (Appendix A) §1.3, §1.6; Compatibility & Versioning row "C (compressed)"; ADR-3 Add 16-bit re-expansion in ooo-frontend::fetch.rs before ooo-isa::decode(); fetch-width accounting (§4.6) must switch from fixed 4-byte to variable-length granularity.
DF4 Full RV64GC (umbrella of DF1–DF3) PhaseDeferred("FINAL_BLUEPRINT §1.6") (§1.3 NON-goals table, "Full RV64GC" row) §1.6 Deferred features table; ADR-3 rationale + rejected-alternative column Resolves automatically once DF1+DF2+DF3 all land; no independent work beyond their sum.
DF5 Multi-core / cache coherence (MESI) Stated NON-GOAL (§1.3, §4.6); ADR-26 §1.3; ADR-26 "When the Rejected Choice Wins"; Threat Model "Out-of-scope" note Never in this project by charter — owned entirely by project 187 (MESI Coherence); this simulator's ooo-mem boundary is the seam project 187 would extend.
DF6 Full M/S/U privileged mode + virtual memory / page tables §1.3 NON-goals; ADR-25; Threat Model "Out-of-scope (state explicitly)" note §1.3; ADR-25 "When the Rejected Choice Wins"; §21 Tlb page-walk stub Implement the full CSR ladder (satp, sstatus, …), a real page-table walker, and interrupt delivery — required only if the simulator must boot an OS/RTOS (ADR-25's rejected-choice regime).
DF7 Cache/memory prefetchers Repo-tree NON-GOALS note, §21 (Phase 9) §21 Phase 9 Objectives/Pitfalls; Scalability Analysis memory-scaling note Add a stream/stride prefetcher issuing speculative MemRequests into the MSHR path of ooo-mem::cache.rs; must not perturb determinism (§4.4) or the cosim oracle (§6.1).
DF8 Aggressive speculative memory-dependence prediction (store-set predictor) PhaseDeferred toggle note, §20 (Phase 8, 20.2); ADR-14 rejected-alternative column §20 Phase 8; ADR-14; P10 (Chrysos & Emer, store-set prediction) Implement speculate-past-unknown-store + commit-time violation detect (P10's store-set predictor) as an opt-in ConfigParams toggle alongside the conservative default (ADR-14).
DF9 Full TAGE-SC-L predictor (beyond simplified TageLite) Repo-tree scope comment on ooo-frontend/tage.rs (Appendix A); P4 reference note §15 Phase 3; P4 (Seznec & Michaud / Seznec TAGE-SC-L); ADR-11 Extend TageLite's tagged-geometric-history core with the loop predictor + statistical corrector (SC) components P4 describes; deferred because TageLite's simplified tagged-component already meets the ≥95% accuracy target (§4.7) on the reference microbenchmark suite.

All nine entries are re-checked at every god-pass harness run (H16): each citation above must continue to resolve to an existing heading/row, and no entry may be silently dropped from this register while its PhaseDeferred call or scope note still exists in the body text.

Glossary

Terms ordered roughly by pipeline flow, then cross-cutting concepts. Each entry is precise + tied to this simulator's types/crates.

Term Definition (implementation-relevant)
Out-of-order (OoO) execution Instructions issue/execute as soon as their operands ready, not in fetch order; reordering hidden by in-order commit. This sim's defining property.
Superscalar >1 instruction per stage per cycle; widths set by ConfigParams.fetch_width/issue_width/commit_width (Phase 11).
Cycle-accurate Models per-cycle state transitions, not just functional results; each tick() = one clock. Distinct from functional-only golden model.
ISA vs microarchitecture ISA = RV64IM contract (regs/mem/PC, in ooo-isa); microarchitecture = how it's realized (ROB/RS/LSQ/renaming). Sim implements one µarch of the ISA.
Architectural register ISA-visible x0..x31 in ArchState.xregs; what commit must reproduce. x0 hardwired 0.
Physical register PhysReg(u16) in PhysRegFile; renaming target. #phys = ConfigParams.phys_regs > 32, enabling in-flight versions.
Register renaming Map arch→phys (Rat) to eliminate WAR/WAW false deps; only true RAW remain. RenameUnit, Phase 4. x0 never renamed.
RAT (register alias table) Rat { map: [PhysReg; 32] }; current arch→phys mapping. Snapshotted into RenameCheckpoint per branch.
Free list FreeList; queue of unallocated PhysReg. Pop on rename-dst; push old_phys on commit. Empty → rename stall.
Reorder buffer (ROB) ReorderBuffer circular entries/head/tail; RobEntry per in-flight inst. Allocated in-order at dispatch, retired in-order at commit.
In-order commit CommitUnit retires up to commit_width head entries/cycle in program order; only point arch state mutates. Guarantees preciseness.
Precise exception On trap (RobEntry.exception), state reflects all older insts committed, none younger. Squash younger ROB/RS/LSQ at head. Phase 10.
Reservation station (RS) RsEntry buffer holding waiting ops + operand-ready bits until issued. Here a unified Scheduler.entries.
Unified scheduler Single shared Scheduler (vs per-FU RS); holds all RsEntry, does wakeup + select across issue ports. Phase 6.
Wakeup Completing FU broadcasts its dst PhysReg; matching RsEntry.src1/src2 set *_ready. CAM-style match.
Select Per issue port, pick oldest ready RsEntry (!issued, both srcs ready), mark issued. Age-ordered arbitration.
Issue width Max insts issued to FUs/cycle = ConfigParams.issue_width; bounded by #IssuePort.
Dispatch Post-rename insertion of inst into ROB (RobEntry) + RS (RsEntry) in-order. Stalls on ROB/RS full (structural). Phase 5.
Functional unit (FU) FunctionalUnit { kind: FuKind, latency, pipelined, busy_until }. FuKind: Alu/Mul/Div/Branch/Agu/LoadStore.
Bypass / forwarding network BypassNetwork forwards ExecResult to dependent issue same/next cycle, so back-to-back dependent ALU ops skip full writeback latency.
Writeback (WB) FU result written to PhysRegFile.regs, ready[dst]=true; triggers wakeup. Distinct from commit.
Load/store queue (LSQ) LoadStoreQueue { loads, stores } of LsqEntry; tracks addr/value/ready/committed for memory ordering. Phase 8.
Memory disambiguation Determine load↔store address aliasing. Unknown older-store addr → load waits (conservative) or speculates + checks at commit.
Store-to-load forwarding Load with exact-address match to older in-flight store reads that store's value directly, bypassing cache.
Speculation Execute past unresolved branches / speculative loads; results held in phys regs/LSQ, squashed if wrong. Never mutates arch state pre-commit.
Branch prediction Predict direction + target at fetch to avoid stall. BranchPredictor trait: predict/update. Phase 3.
BTB (branch target buffer) Btb; set-associative pc→target cache for taken branches/jumps. Supplies target before decode.
RAS (return address stack) Ras; LIFO of return addresses; push on Jal(call), pop on Jalr(return) for accurate return prediction.
gshare Gshare; global history XOR pc indexes 2-bit saturating counters for direction. Default predictor.
TAGE TageLite; tagged geometric-history-length predictor; multiple tables, longest-matching-tag wins. Higher accuracy alt to gshare.
Misprediction penalty Cycles lost from wrong-path fetch until redirect after branch resolves in execute; cost = pipeline depth + recovery. Counted in Stats.mispredicts.
Squash / flush Discard wrong-path / post-trap entries: invalidate ROB/RS/LSQ younger than offending inst, redirect fetch.
Checkpoint / recovery RenameCheckpoint { rat, free_head } captured at branch rename; on mispredict, restore RAT + roll back FreeList, then squash.
Structural hazard Resource exhaustion (ROB/RS/LSQ full, FU busy_until busy, free list empty) forcing a stall/bubble.
Data hazard (RAW/WAR/WAW) RAW = true dep (tracked via PhysReg readiness); WAR/WAW = false deps eliminated by renaming.
IPC (instructions per cycle) Stats.committed as f64 / Stats.cycles. Primary perf metric; >1 only achievable superscalar.
Pipeline latch Inter-stage register; modeled by reverse-order tick() so a value produced cycle C is consumed only C+1.
Stall / bubble Stall = stage holds (no advance); bubble = injected no-op slot. Caused by hazards; reduce IPC.
Golden model ooo-isa functional execute_functional; reference architectural behavior, timing-independent.
Co-simulation Lock-step compare committed ArchState (timing model) vs golden every commit; mismatch = divergence. Phase 12.
Deterministic replay Same seed/ConfigParams ⇒ bit-identical cycle-by-cycle run; enables divergence bisection.
Cache Cache; set-associative L1/L2 with MSHRs + miss latency; feeds memory latency into issue/wakeup. Phase 9.
MSHR (miss status holding register) Tracks outstanding cache misses; enables hit-under-miss / multiple in-flight misses (non-blocking cache).
TLB (translation lookaside buffer) Tlb; set-associative VA→PA cache; miss adds latency to AGU/load path.
CSR (control/status register) CsrFile in ArchState; minimal subset for traps; full privileged mode is a non-goal.
Trap RobEntry.exception: Option<Trap>; precise fault/interrupt resolved at commit, triggering squash + handler redirect.
Tick (reverse-order) OooCore::tick() advances stages Commit→WB→Execute→Issue→Dispatch→Rename→Decode→Fetch; load-bearing determinism rule preventing same-cycle teleporting.

Every leaf file (and every leaf directory the tree does not further expand) from the §10 Repository Layout tree, one-line purpose each, names matching the tree and the Core Schema (§11) exactly. Crate = Cargo workspace member; oooc is the single binary.

fuzz/Cargo.toml libfuzzer-sys harness crate.
fuzz/fuzz_targets/decode.rs Arbitrary u32 → decode never panics; P1.
fuzz/fuzz_targets/cosim.rs Random program → OooCore commits == golden; never diverge; P12.
fuzz/fuzz_targets/recover.rs Inject mispredicts → RAT/FreeList/ROB invariants hold post-flush; P10.

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

One row per phase (## 12–## 25). Durations sum to the Section 1 timeline (28 weeks). All exit criteria are measurable + gate the next phase; co-sim equivalence (committed arch-state == execute_functional) is the universal correctness invariant from Phase 12 onward.

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
0 (## 12) Workspace: nine ooo-* crates, Cycle/ConfigParams/Stats shells, OooCore::tick() stub, CI + Makefile + Dockerfile; smoke sum 1..N via ooo-isa golden model 1 wk Crate dependency cycle; no_std+alloc boundary in ooo-isa leaks std; CI not deterministic cargo build --workspace + make test green; oooc run sum.hex prints correct Σ; nine crates link in locked dep DAG; CI reproducible (pinned MSRV 1.78)
1 (## 13) RV64IM decode() + ArchState + execute_functional() golden model: full RV64I + M, x0-zero, sign/zero-ext, trap skeleton 2 wk Sign-extension off-by-one (lw/addiw); mulh/div signedness + div-by-zero/overflow per spec; immediate field mis-extract All riscv-tests rv64ui + rv64um pass on golden model; 100% opcode decode coverage; div-by-0 → defined result (not panic), overflow per ISA manual
2 (## 14) In-order 5-stage scaffold: stage structs, reverse-order tick(), pipeline latches, stall/bubble 2 wk Same-cycle teleport (forward-order bug); latch double-update; stall logic deadlock Reverse-order invariant test: value at cycle C visible only C+1; in-order CPI ≈ ideal + measured stalls; no deadlock over riscv-tests
3 (## 15) Fetch + BPred: FetchUnit/FetchBuffer, Btb, Ras, Gshare→TageLite, Prediction, resolve-redirect 2.5 wk RAS corruption on speculative push/pop; gshare history not checkpointed; BTB aliasing Gshare ≥ 90% / TageLite ≥ 95% direction accuracy on branch-heavy bench; RAS ≥ 98% on call/ret; redirect latency = configured penalty
4 (## 16) Decode/Rename: Rat, FreeList, PhysReg, PhysRegFile, RenameUnit, RenameCheckpoint 2 wk x0 erroneously renamed; free-list leak/double-free of PhysReg; checkpoint not capturing free_head Zero WAR/WAW stalls observed; free-list conservation (allocated+free == phys_regs) invariant holds every cycle; x0 always reads 0
5 (## 17) Dispatch → ReorderBuffer: alloc RobEntry+phys dst, record old_phys, in-order alloc, ROB-full stall 1.5 wk head/tail circular wrap bug; old_phys not recorded → commit can't free; alloc out of order In-order alloc invariant; ROB-full back-pressure stalls dispatch (no overrun); old_phys recorded for every renamed dst
6 (## 18) Issue scheduler: Scheduler/RsEntry, wakeup broadcast, oldest-ready select per FU, OoO issue 2.5 wk Wakeup misses a matching src; select not age-ordered (starvation); double-issue RAW-only stalls; wakeup→issue ≤ 1 cycle; oldest-ready select verified by age order; issued flag prevents re-issue; OoO issue demonstrably reorders vs program order
7 (## 19) Execute: FunctionalUnit set (Alu/Mul/Div/Branch/Agu), latency table, pipelined vs iterative FUs, BypassNetwork, writeback 2 wk Iterative FU (Div) busy_until accounting; bypass timing vs writeback race; latency table wrong Latencies match table (Alu 1, Mul 3, Div ~20+, etc.); back-to-back dependent Alu = 1 cyc apart via bypass; pipelined FU accepts 1/cyc, iterative blocks
8 (## 20) Memory pipeline: LoadStoreQueue/LsqEntry, AGU addr-gen, disambiguation, store-to-load forwarding, ROB ordering 2.5 wk Forwarding wrong/stale store; unknown older-store addr handling; partial-overlap forward Exact-addr STLF forwards correct value; unknown older store → conservative wait; loads/stores ordered vs ROB; cosim clean on mem-heavy tests
9 (## 21) Cache + TLB: set-assoc L1/L2, MSHRs, miss latency, Tlb, latency feedback into wakeup 2 wk MSHR exhaustion stall; miss latency not feeding wakeup timing; TLB miss not stalling L1/L2 hit/miss latencies as configured; MSHR limit back-pressures; variable load latency correctly delays dependent wakeup; cosim unaffected (timing-only)
10 (## 22) Commit + precise exceptions + recovery: in-order CommitUnit, free old_phys, traps, mispredict flush, RenameCheckpoint restore, LSQ squash 2.5 wk Leaked PhysReg on flush; partial squash (younger-than-branch boundary); trap not precise Precise state at every trap == golden; flush restores RAT/free-list/ROB/RS/LSQ to pre-branch; free-list conservation survives 10⁶ mispredicts; LSQ squash exact
11 (## 23) Superscalar widening: multi-wide fetch/rename/issue/commit, multiple FUs + IssuePorts, port contention, full Stats set 2 wk Multi-port wakeup CAM scaling; rename N dsts/cycle correctness; commit-width vs ROB head IPC scales with width on ILP-rich bench (≥ 1.5× from 1→2-wide); port contention modeled (Stats); all perf counters populated + cross-checked
12 (## 24) Golden co-sim + determinism: lock-step commit compare vs execute_functional, seeded replay, divergence detect + bisection 1.5 wk Hidden nondeterminism (HashMap iter, time); divergence not localized Every committed entry matches golden arch-state; identical seed/config → bit-identical trace across runs; injected bug auto-bisected to cycle + ROB idx
13 (## 25) Conformance + differential + fuzz + perf: riscv-tests, diff vs Spike, random-program fuzzer, IPC/perf benches 2 wk Fuzzer finds rare disambiguation/recovery divergence; Spike trace-format mismatch 100% riscv-tests rv64ui/um pass; zero divergence vs Spike on test corpus; ≥ 10⁶ fuzzed instrs with no cosim divergence; sim throughput ≥ 1M cycles/s; IPC within ±10% of published microbench expectations

Total: 28 weeks, matching Section 1's timeline.

Phase-0 done = workspace compiles, nine crates link, oooc run executes a trivial program through the ooo-isa golden model and prints the correct result, CI/Makefile/Dockerfile green — a functional skeleton with a stubbed tick(), no timing model yet.

Full done = all 14 phases complete: a cycle-accurate superscalar OoO RV64IM core whose committed architectural state is verified bit-identical to the ooo-isa golden model on every commit (Phase 12), passes 100% of riscv-tests and differential testing vs Spike with zero divergence over ≥ 10⁶ fuzzed instructions (Phase 13), sustains ≥ 1M cycles/s with deterministic seeded replay, and reports IPC + microarchitectural Stats within ±10% of published microbenchmark expectations.
